Now I have all the information needed to analyze each of the 6 issues. Let me go through them:

### Issue 1: Abstract puts "4 depths benchmark" and "EDD=4" together, misleading

**Abstract line 4**: "...four depths under controlled full-replacement chains at the 1--1.5B parameter scale. A lightweight pipeline ... with the Effective Discriminable Depth (EDD0.70) reaching 4 for all three families."

The user's concern: The main benchmark is d0-d3 (4 depths), so the maximum testable EDD from the primary benchmark alone would be 3 (since you can only test d0v1, d1v2, d2v3 = 3 adjacent pairs). 

EDD=4 actually comes from extending the depth range to d0-d5 on the nucleus model, where they test additional pairs like d3v4 and find they all exceed the T=0.70 threshold. Looking at the EDD definition more carefully—EDD_T is the maximum d*+1 where all adjacent pairs up to d* meet the threshold—I see that with depths {0,1,2,3}, you can only achieve EDD=3 at most. To reach EDD=4, you'd need to test a fourth adjacent pair, which requires going beyond the main benchmark's depth range.

The abstract mentions "four depths" for the benchmark but then claims "EDD reaching 4," which is misleading since that actually requires the extended 5-depth validation on the nucleus cells alone. The Introduction clarifies this comes from the extension, but the abstract glosses over this distinction, making it sound like EDD=4 emerges from the standard benchmark when it doesn't.

Now looking at Qwen2.5-7B's reported "EDD > 5" — if the depth set only goes to 5, then the maximum d* is 4 (testing the pair at depths 4 and 5), which caps EDD at 5, not exceeding it. The claim doesn't align with how EDD is actually defined. I'm realizing the definition constrains d* to {0,...,4} when K=5, so the maximum EDD value is 5—representing all five consecutive pairs exceeding the threshold. Claiming EDD > 5 would require evidence that d5v6 also exceeds T, which the data doesn't provide since testing only goes up to depth 5. I can see the intuition behind the mistake, but the notation is definitively wrong here. might write ">5" - they mean "at least 5, and possibly higher if we tested further." But per the formal definition, the correct value is EDD=5 given the tested depth range.

Actually, the user's point is more subtle. They say "EDD > 5" is wrong because you haven't tested d5-vs-d6. The maximum you can say is EDD ≥ 5 or simply EDD = 5 (the max observable from the tested range). Writing ">5" is indeed an error because it asserts something about untested depths.

**Verdict: Real problem.** Should be "EDD = 5" (the maximum observable from depths 0-5), not "EDD > 5".

### Issue 3: "sole non-monotonic case" contradicts Appendix B.14

Looking at method.tex line 100, it claims there's only one non-monotonic case, but Appendix B.14 actually shows multiple: Qwen2.5-7B has a U-shaped pattern with EDD > 5, and Llama-3.1-8B base shows non-monotonic AUC decay with EDD=2. None of these have EDD=0, so the claim about a sole non-monotonic case yielding EDD=0 doesn't match what's documented. Let me reread the context more carefully to see if the statement is scoped to just the primary benchmark subset. Ah, I see the confusion now. The method.tex statement about "the sole non-monotonic case" is actually referring to Qwen2.5-14B with self-reference, which shows increasing performance across scales (d0v1=0.643, d1v2=0.713, d2v3=0.728) and hits EDD=0 because the first value falls below the 0.70 threshold. So that's the specific instruction-tuned model outside the primary atlas they're pointing to, not a claim that it's the only non-monotonic case overall.

But looking at the full appendix data, there are actually multiple non-monotonic cases outside the primary atlas—Qwen2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B base also show U-shaped patterns—so calling it "the sole" case is inaccurate. And these other cases don't all have EDD=0 either, which contradicts the implication in the methods section.

Then there's another tension: theory.tex claims EDD should decrease with scale because larger models approximate the training distribution more faithfully, but Qwen2.5-7B has EDD=5, which seems to contradict this scaling prediction.

Now I'm noticing inconsistencies in how the classifier comparison is reported across the paper—the intro and results sections cite different precision point margins for when RF, XGBoost, and LightGBM converge. I'm realizing these might be two different evaluation settings—the 0.4 pp could refer to in-distribution performance while the 0.8 pp refers to cross-model transfer. But the intro doesn't specify which setting it's claiming, and Table 4 only shows the RF row without separate XGBoost or LightGBM entries to verify the 0.4 pp claim at all.

Now looking at the CORAL and DivEye claim in the same section—it says they match within 0.6 pp on Pythia nucleus, but Table 4 doesn't actually include those methods either, just RF and Binoculars. So this is another claim without direct table support in the main paper. I'm noticing that CORAL and DivEye are mentioned in the text as matching within 0.6 percentage points on Pythia nucleus results, but they don't appear as standalone rows in Table 4—CORAL only shows up as a calibration method applied to Min-K% Prob. This is a genuine gap between what's claimed and what's actually presented in the results. So that's another real problem to add to my list of issues with this paper.

    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nAs language models increasingly train on web text containing their own outputs, a recursive model$\\to$data$\\to$model loop arises, yet no method estimates how many such iterations a text has undergone.\nWe formalize \\emph{recursive generation depth estimation} as ordinal classification and construct a 180K-passage benchmark spanning three model families, three decoding strategies, and four depths under controlled full-replacement chains at the 1--1.5B parameter scale.\nA lightweight pipeline (19 statistical features dominated by perplexity tail statistics, classified by Random Forest) achieves d1--d3 balanced accuracy of 59--82\\% across nine model--strategy cells, with the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}$) reaching 4 for all three families.\nThree findings emerge: (1)~depth signal concentrates in perplexity distribution tail statistics, confirmed by classifier convergence and independent cross-perplexity features (Binoculars, 78.1\\% vs.\\ 78.4\\%); (2)~decoding strategy affects cross-condition transfer more than model architecture (d1--d3: 45.9\\% cross-strategy vs.\\ 55.4\\% cross-model), suggesting structurally distinct degradation trajectories; (3)~signal attenuates with scale: at 14B, accuracy drops to ${\\sim}44$\\% under cross-reference ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$), with reference-model choice accounting for ${\\sim}7$\\,pp of the apparent degradation; depth estimation claims hold most strongly within-strategy or when the decoding configuration is known.\nPerturbation-based and membership-inference baselines (29--33\\%) confirm that binary detection scores lack ordinal depth resolution.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

411	
412	\paragraph{(1) Depth signal persists at 7--8B scale.}
413	Both 7--8B models substantially exceed the 25\% chance baseline: Llama-3.1-8B achieves 70.2\% (95\% CI $[68.3, 72.4]$) and Qwen2.5-7B achieves 60.1\% ($[58.0, 62.1]$); recursive depth therefore remains estimable from perplexity-based features even when the generator is 5--7$\times$ larger than the reference model.
414	Depth-0 separability is preserved in both models: d0v1 AUC is 0.949 (Llama-3.1) and 0.969 (Qwen2.5).
415	
416	\paragraph{(2) EDD at 7--8B scale.}
417	At threshold $T{=}0.70$, Qwen2.5-7B maintains all consecutive-pair AUCs above 0.70 through depth~5 (Table~\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}), yielding $\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70} > 5$, exceeding the $\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$ observed at 1--1.5B.
418	Llama-3.1-8B matches the smaller models at $\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$: its monotonic decay causes d4v5 (0.618) to drop below $T$, despite stronger signal at shallower depths.
419	Both larger generators produce weaker per-step signal than the 1--1.5B models (mean consecutive AUC: 0.786 for Llama-3.1, 0.782 for Qwen2.5, vs.\ 0.852 at 1--1.5B).
420	
421	\begin{table}[h]

      "content": "\\midrule\nGPT-2~XL (1.5B) & nucleus & 84.3 & 4 & 0.999 \\\\\nOLMo-1B & nucleus & 78.9 & 4 & 0.997 \\\\\nPythia-1.4B & nucleus & 78.7 & 4 & 0.998 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLlama-3.1-8B & nucleus & 70.2\\,[68.3, 72.4] & 4 & 0.949 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & nucleus & 60.1\\,[58.0, 62.1] & ${>}5$ & 0.969 \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen2.5-14B$^\\dagger$ & nucleus & 54.1\\,[53.4, 54.7] & 0 & 0.643 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B$^\\dagger$ & temp09 & 50.5\\,[49.8, 51.2] & 0 & 0.632 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B$^\\dagger$ & top-$k$50 & 47.9\\,[47.3, 48.6] & 0 & 0.561 \\\\\n\\cmidrule{2-5}\n\\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\\textit{14B mean}} & \\textit{50.8} & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{(1) Depth signal persists at 7--8B scale.}\nBoth 7--8B models substantially exceed the 25\\% chance baseline: Llama-3.1-8B achieves 70.2\\% (95\\% CI $[68.3, 72.4]$) and Qwen2.5-7B achieves 60.1\\% ($[58.0, 62.1]$); recursive depth therefore remains estimable from perplexity-based features even when the generator is 5--7$\\times$ larger than the reference model.\nDepth-0 separability is preserved in both models: d0v1 AUC is 0.949 (Llama-3.1) and 0.969 (Qwen2.5).\n\n\\paragraph{(2) EDD at 7--8B scale.}\nAt threshold $T{=}0.70$, Qwen2.5-7B maintains all consecutive-pair AUCs above 0.70 through depth~5 (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_scale_auc}), yielding $\\mathrm{EDD}_{T{=}0.70} > 5$, exceeding the $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$ observed at 1--1.5B.\nLlama-3.1-8B matches the smaller models at $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}4$: its monotonic decay causes d4v5 (0.618) to drop below $T$, despite stronger signal at shallower depths.\nBoth larger generators produce weaker per-step signal than the 1--1.5B models (mean consecutive AUC: 0.786 for Llama-3.1, 0.782 for Qwen2.5, vs.\\ 0.852 at 1--1.5B).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Consecutive binary AUC. Llama-3.1-8B exhibits monotonic decay; Qwen2.5-7B shows a U-shaped pattern; Qwen2.5-14B$^\\dagger$ (depths 0--3 only) has all pairs below $T{=}0.70$ with strategy-specific patterns. $^\\dagger$Self-reference model.}",
      "numLines": 30,

      "totalLines": 932
    }

95	\end{multline}
96	where $d^* \in \{0, \dots, K{-}1\}$. If\, $\mathrm{AUC}(0,1) < T$, then $\mathrm{EDD}_T = 0$.
97	\end{definition}
98	
99	$\mathrm{EDD}_T$ reports the longest \emph{contiguous} discriminable prefix: the deepest $d$ such that every pair from $(0,1)$ through $(d{-}1, d)$ exceeds $T$.
100	All AUC profiles in our primary benchmark (9 cells $\times$ depths 0--3 plus 3 nucleus cells $\times$ depths 0--5 $=$ 12 profiles) decay monotonically, empirically validating the monotonicity assumption; the sole non-monotonic case (an instruction-tuned model outside the primary atlas; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_scale_full}) yields $\mathrm{EDD}{=}0$, rendering the distinction moot for our configurations.
101	We adopt $T{=}0.70$ following the ``acceptable discrimination'' threshold in ROC analysis \cite{hosmer2000} (8/9 cells achieve $\mathrm{EDD} \geq 3$); sensitivity analysis (\S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}) shows EDD shifts by at most one level across $T \in \{0.65, 0.70, 0.80\}$ in all nine cells.
102	
103	\paragraph{Classifiers.}
104	Our primary classifier is a Random Forest (200 trees, unconstrained depth, 5-fold CV; EDD binary classifiers use 500 trees).\footnote{Statistical tests are not corrected for multiple comparisons; $p$-values should be interpreted as exploratory.}
105	The RF produces ordinally consistent predictions: 98.2\% of errors fall on adjacent depths ($|\Delta| \leq 1$), quadratic weighted kappa (QWK)\,=\,0.89; CORAL ordinal regression matches within 0.6\,pp ($p > 0.05$; \S\ref{sec:generalization}).
106	Comparisons against CORAL \cite{coral2020}, DivEye \cite{diveye2026}, and other baselines appear in \S\ref{sec:generalization}.

    "newString": "All AUC profiles in our primary benchmark (9 cells $\\times$ depths 0--3 plus 3 nucleus cells $\\times$ depths 0--5 $=$ 12 profiles) decay monotonically; non-monotonic profiles (U-shaped recovery in Qwen2.5-7B, non-monotonic decay in Llama-3.1-8B base; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}) occur only in cross-scale extensions outside the primary atlas.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Definition}\n\\label{sec:method:problem}\n\nLet $f_\\theta$ denote a language model and $\\mathcal{D}_0$ a corpus of human-written seed text.\nWe define a \\emph{recursive generation chain} of depth $K$ as the sequence $\\mathcal{D}_0, \\mathcal{D}_1, \\dots, \\mathcal{D}_K$, where each $\\mathcal{D}_k$ ($k \\geq 1$) is produced by finetuning $f_\\theta$ on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ and sampling from the resulting model.\nDepth $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text; depth $d{=}k$ ($k \\geq 1$) denotes text generated by a model trained on depth-$(k{-}1)$ data.\n\nGiven a text passage $x$, the task is to estimate its recursive generation depth $d \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$.\nThis is an ordinal classification problem: depths carry a natural ordering, and $d{=}1$ is structurally closer to $d{=}0$ than $d{=}3$ is, since each generation step compounds distributional drift.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Generational Benchmark Construction}\n\\label{sec:method:benchmark}\n\nWe construct a benchmark spanning three axes: model family, decoding strategy, and generation depth.\n\n\\paragraph{Model families.}\nWe select three 1--1.5B-parameter models with diverse pretraining corpora and architectures: Pythia-1.4B (EleutherAI), OLMo-1B (AI2), and GPT-2~XL (OpenAI, 1.5B).\nRestricting to a narrow parameter range isolates the effect of architecture and training data from scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding strategies.}\nEach model is paired with three decoding strategies: nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) \\cite{holtzman2020_nucleus}, temperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$), and top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$).\nThese span the dominant modes of open-ended generation and induce qualitatively different entropy profiles.\nOur findings on feature importance and discriminability are limited to this grid; transfer to untested models, scales, or strategies is not established.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth and scale.}\nThe primary benchmark covers depths d0--d3 across all $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells, with 5{,}000 samples per depth per cell (180K samples total).\nFor depth extension experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}), we extend one pipeline (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus) to d0--d5.\n\n\\paragraph{Generation pipeline.}\nStarting from a Wikipedia subset ($\\mathcal{D}_0$), each cycle: (1)~LoRA finetuning \\cite{hu2022lora} on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, lr $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, effective batch 16, 3 epochs, dropout $0.05$; fresh adapter from the base model at each depth; full protocol in Appendix~\\ref{app:lora_protocol}); (2)~sampling 5{,}000 passages (max 512 tokens, beginning-of-sequence (BOS) seeded) under the specified strategy; (3)~$\\mathcal{D}_k$ feeds the next cycle.\nEach depth is produced by a model trained on prior-depth data, not by sampling from the unmodified base model.\nBeginning-of-sequence seeding may amplify depth signal relative to prompted generation; prompted-generation validation (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) confirms signal persistence albeit attenuated ($-$13.8\\,pp).\nNo per-passage lineage exists across depths; cross-validation splits do not introduce leakage.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model.}\nAll statistical features (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) use Pythia-1.4B as fixed reference model.\nThis overlaps with one generator (Pythia), conferring a 4--11\\,pp self-reference advantage on that pipeline (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}); Pythia results in Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} are marked $^\\dagger$ accordingly.\nAlternative references yield 67--79\\%, all exceeding $2.5{\\times}$ chance (Limitations).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Feature Design}\n\\label{sec:method:features}\n\nWe extract a feature vector $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ comprising 19 candidate features from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$; ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) shows the top 5 achieve 97.6\\% of full-set performance, and variance inflation analysis reveals VIF${>}$10 for 14--16 features; effective independent dimensionality is ${\\approx}$4--5.\nThe features fall into four categories (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{The 19 statistical features comprising $\\mathbf{f}(x)$, grouped by category. All perplexity and surprisal features are computed from the reference model (Pythia-1.4B).}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCategory & Features & $n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPerplexity & mean, var, skewness, kurtosis, & 9 \\\\\nstatistics & p10, p25, p50, p75, p90 & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSurprisal & mean, var, entropy & 3 \\\\\nstatistics & & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLexical & type--token ratio, hapax ratio, & 4 \\\\\ndiversity & bigram entropy, trigram entropy & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nRepetition & repeated 2/3/4-gram fractions & 3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\emph{Perplexity statistics} (9 features) capture the per-token perplexity distribution via moments and quantiles (p10--p90); tail quantiles are especially informative as recursive finetuning erodes the high-perplexity tail (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\n\\emph{Surprisal statistics} (3 features): mean, variance, and entropy of $-\\log p(\\text{token})$.\n\\emph{Lexical diversity} (4 features): type--token ratio, hapax ratio, bigram/trigram entropy.\n\\emph{Repetition} (3 features): repeated $n$-gram fractions for $n \\in \\{2, 3, 4\\}$.\n\n\\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}\n\\label{sec:method:edd}\n\nAggregate multi-class accuracy obscures whether discriminability degrades at specific depth boundaries.\nWe propose the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) as an operational measure of how far into a recursive chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\n\\paragraph{Pairwise discriminability.}\nLet $\\mathcal{D} = \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$ denote the depth set and $T \\in (0.5, 1.0]$ a discrimination threshold.\nFor each adjacent pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we define the pairwise discriminability $\\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ as the area under the ROC curve of a dedicated binary Random Forest classifier (500 trees, $\\lfloor\\sqrt{19}\\rfloor$ candidate features per split) trained on class-balanced data from $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) to distinguish depth-$d$ from depth-$(d{+}1)$ samples, evaluated via 5-fold stratified cross-validation.\nEach pair uses an independent classifier to avoid decision boundary dilution from multi-class settings.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Effective Discriminable Depth]\n\\label{def:edd}\nThe \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} at threshold $T$ is:\n\\begin{multline}\n\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max \\bigl\\{ d^*{+}1 \\,:\\, \\\\\n\\forall\\, d' \\leq d^*,\\; \\mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \\geq T \\bigr\\}\n\\label{eq:edd}\n\\end{multline}\nwhere $d^* \\in \\{0, \\dots, K{-}1\\}$. If\\, $\\mathrm{AUC}(0,1) < T$, then $\\mathrm{EDD}_T = 0$.\n\\end{definition}\n\n$\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ reports the longest \\emph{contiguous} discriminable prefix: the deepest $d$ such that every pair from $(0,1)$ through $(d{-}1, d)$ exceeds $T$.\nAll AUC profiles in our primary benchmark (9 cells $\\times$ depths 0--3 plus 3 nucleus cells $\\times$ depths 0--5 $=$ 12 profiles) decay monotonically, empirically validating the monotonicity assumption; the sole non-monotonic case (an instruction-tuned model outside the primary atlas; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}) yields $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}0$, rendering the distinction moot for our configurations.\nWe adopt $T{=}0.70$ following the ``acceptable discrimination'' threshold in ROC analysis \\cite{hosmer2000} (8/9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$); sensitivity analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}) shows EDD shifts by at most one level across $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.80\\}$ in all nine cells.\n\n\\paragraph{Classifiers.}\nOur primary classifier is a Random Forest (200 trees, unconstrained depth, 5-fold CV; EDD binary classifiers use 500 trees).\\footnote{Statistical tests are not corrected for multiple comparisons; $p$-values should be interpreted as exploratory.}\nThe RF produces ordinally consistent predictions: 98.2\\% of errors fall on adjacent depths ($|\\Delta| \\leq 1$), quadratic weighted kappa (QWK)\\,=\\,0.89; CORAL ordinal regression matches within 0.6\\,pp ($p > 0.05$; \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\nComparisons against CORAL \\cite{coral2020}, DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}, and other baselines appear in \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}.",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Definition}\n\\label{sec:method:problem}\n\nLet $f_\\theta$ denote a language model and $\\mathcal{D}_0$ a corpus of human-written seed text.\nWe define a \\emph{recursive generation chain} of depth $K$ as the sequence $\\mathcal{D}_0, \\mathcal{D}_1, \\dots, \\mathcal{D}_K$, where each $\\mathcal{D}_k$ ($k \\geq 1$) is produced by finetuning $f_\\theta$ on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ and sampling from the resulting model.\nDepth $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text; depth $d{=}k$ ($k \\geq 1$) denotes text generated by a model trained on depth-$(k{-}1)$ data.\n\nGiven a text passage $x$, the task is to estimate its recursive generation depth $d \\in \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$.\nThis is an ordinal classification problem: depths carry a natural ordering, and $d{=}1$ is structurally closer to $d{=}0$ than $d{=}3$ is, since each generation step compounds distributional drift.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Generational Benchmark Construction}\n\\label{sec:method:benchmark}\n\nWe construct a benchmark spanning three axes: model family, decoding strategy, and generation depth.\n\n\\paragraph{Model families.}\nWe select three 1--1.5B-parameter models with diverse pretraining corpora and architectures: Pythia-1.4B (EleutherAI), OLMo-1B (AI2), and GPT-2~XL (OpenAI, 1.5B).\nRestricting to a narrow parameter range isolates the effect of architecture and training data from scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding strategies.}\nEach model is paired with three decoding strategies: nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) \\cite{holtzman2020_nucleus}, temperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$), and top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$).\nThese span the dominant modes of open-ended generation and induce qualitatively different entropy profiles.\nOur findings on feature importance and discriminability are limited to this grid; transfer to untested models, scales, or strategies is not established.\n\n\\paragraph{Depth and scale.}\nThe primary benchmark covers depths d0--d3 across all $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells, with 5{,}000 samples per depth per cell (180K samples total).\nFor depth extension experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}), we extend one pipeline (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus) to d0--d5.\n\n\\paragraph{Generation pipeline.}\nStarting from a Wikipedia subset ($\\mathcal{D}_0$), each cycle: (1)~LoRA finetuning \\cite{hu2022lora} on $\\mathcal{D}_{k-1}$ ($r{=}16$, $\\alpha{=}32$, lr $2{\\times}10^{-4}$, effective batch 16, 3 epochs, dropout $0.05$; fresh adapter from the base model at each depth; full protocol in Appendix~\\ref{app:lora_protocol}); (2)~sampling 5{,}000 passages (max 512 tokens, beginning-of-sequence (BOS) seeded) under the specified strategy; (3)~$\\mathcal{D}_k$ feeds the next cycle.\nEach depth is produced by a model trained on prior-depth data, not by sampling from the unmodified base model.\nBeginning-of-sequence seeding may amplify depth signal relative to prompted generation; prompted-generation validation (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}) confirms signal persistence albeit attenuated ($-$13.8\\,pp).\nNo per-passage lineage exists across depths; cross-validation splits do not introduce leakage.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model.}\nAll statistical features (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) use Pythia-1.4B as fixed reference model.\nThis overlaps with one generator (Pythia), conferring a 4--11\\,pp self-reference advantage on that pipeline (Appendix~\\ref{app:ref_ablation}); Pythia results in Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} are marked $^\\dagger$ accordingly.\nAlternative references yield 67--79\\%, all exceeding $2.5{\\times}$ chance (Limitations).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Feature Design}\n\\label{sec:method:features}\n\nWe extract a feature vector $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ comprising 19 candidate features from the reference-model perplexity distribution over each passage $x$; ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) shows the top 5 achieve 97.6\\% of full-set performance, and variance inflation analysis reveals VIF${>}$10 for 14--16 features; effective independent dimensionality is ${\\approx}$4--5.\nThe features fall into four categories (Table~\\ref{tab:features}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{The 19 statistical features comprising $\\mathbf{f}(x)$, grouped by category. All perplexity and surprisal features are computed from the reference model (Pythia-1.4B).}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCategory & Features & $n$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPerplexity & mean, var, skewness, kurtosis, & 9 \\\\\nstatistics & p10, p25, p50, p75, p90 & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nSurprisal & mean, var, entropy & 3 \\\\\nstatistics & & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLexical & type--token ratio, hapax ratio, & 4 \\\\\ndiversity & bigram entropy, trigram entropy & \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nRepetition & repeated 2/3/4-gram fractions & 3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\emph{Perplexity statistics} (9 features) capture the per-token perplexity distribution via moments and quantiles (p10--p90); tail quantiles are especially informative as recursive finetuning erodes the high-perplexity tail (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}).\n\\emph{Surprisal statistics} (3 features): mean, variance, and entropy of $-\\log p(\\text{token})$.\n\\emph{Lexical diversity} (4 features): type--token ratio, hapax ratio, bigram/trigram entropy.\n\\emph{Repetition} (3 features): repeated $n$-gram fractions for $n \\in \\{2, 3, 4\\}$.\n\n\\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}\n\\label{sec:method:edd}\n\nAggregate multi-class accuracy obscures whether discriminability degrades at specific depth boundaries.\nWe propose the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) as an operational measure of how far into a recursive chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\n\\paragraph{Pairwise discriminability.}\nLet $\\mathcal{D} = \\{0, 1, \\dots, K\\}$ denote the depth set and $T \\in (0.5, 1.0]$ a discrimination threshold.\nFor each adjacent pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we define the pairwise discriminability $\\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$ as the area under the ROC curve of a dedicated binary Random Forest classifier (500 trees, $\\lfloor\\sqrt{19}\\rfloor$ candidate features per split) trained on class-balanced data from $\\mathbf{f}(x)$ (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}) to distinguish depth-$d$ from depth-$(d{+}1)$ samples, evaluated via 5-fold stratified cross-validation.\nEach pair uses an independent classifier to avoid decision boundary dilution from multi-class settings.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Effective Discriminable Depth]\n\\label{def:edd}\nThe \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} at threshold $T$ is:\n\\begin{multline}\n\\mathrm{EDD}_T = \\max \\bigl\\{ d^*{+}1 \\,:\\, \\\\\n\\forall\\, d' \\leq d^*,\\; \\mathrm{AUC}(d', d'{+}1) \\geq T \\bigr\\}\n\\label{eq:edd}\n\\end{multline}\nwhere $d^* \\in \\{0, \\dots, K{-}1\\}$. If\\, $\\mathrm{AUC}(0,1) < T$, then $\\mathrm{EDD}_T = 0$.\n\\end{definition}\n\n$\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ reports the longest \\emph{contiguous} discriminable prefix: the deepest $d$ such that every pair from $(0,1)$ through $(d{-}1, d)$ exceeds $T$.\nAll AUC profiles in our primary benchmark (9 cells $\\times$ depths 0--3 plus 3 nucleus cells $\\times$ depths 0--5 $=$ 12 profiles) decay monotonically; non-monotonic profiles (U-shaped recovery in Qwen2.5-7B, non-monotonic decay in Llama-3.1-8B base; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}) occur only in cross-scale extensions outside the primary atlas.\nWe adopt $T{=}0.70$ following the ``acceptable discrimination'' threshold in ROC analysis \\cite{hosmer2000} (8/9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$); sensitivity analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}) shows EDD shifts by at most one level across $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.80\\}$ in all nine cells.\n\n\\paragraph{Classifiers.}\nOur primary classifier is a Random Forest (200 trees, unconstrained depth, 5-fold CV; EDD binary classifiers use 500 trees).\\footnote{Statistical tests are not corrected for multiple comparisons; $p$-values should be interpreted as exploratory.}\nThe RF produces ordinally consistent predictions: 98.2\\% of errors fall on adjacent depths ($|\\Delta| \\leq 1$), quadratic weighted kappa (QWK)\\,=\\,0.89; CORAL ordinal regression matches within 0.6\\,pp ($p > 0.05$; \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\nComparisons against CORAL \\cite{coral2020}, DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}, and other baselines appear in \\S\\ref{sec:generalization}.",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01A6HmyXRBBJ8xw2i9pzGtdJ`</summary>

```text
1	\subsection{Theoretical Grounding}
2	\label{sec:method:theory}
3	
4	Recursive generation contracts the support of the learned distribution, with low-probability tokens lost first \cite{shumailov2024_nature}: tail mass $P_k(\mathrm{ppl} > t)$ decreases monotonically in depth $k$.
5	This tail erosion predicts the perplexity tail feature family as a depth marker, confirmed by its dominance across all 9 cells (\S\ref{sec:atlas:features}).
6	Surprisal variance captures complementary distributional narrowing: as the support contracts, per-token surprise becomes more predictable.
7	
8	\paragraph{EDD upper bound.}
9	Tail mass at depth $k$ scales as $\alpha^k$ for a contraction factor $\alpha \in (0,1)$ \cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}; EDD reaches its upper bound when adjacent-depth feature distance falls below classifier resolution.
10	Our empirical AUC-excess contraction (ratio 0.80 at d1v2$\to$d2v3, 0.69 at d3v4$\to$d4v5; \S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}) is consistent with geometric decay approaching a fixed point \cite{bertrand2024_stability}.
11	5-class extensions to d4 (71--80\%, chance = 20\%; Appendix~\ref{app:depth4_ext}) confirm depth~4 discriminability; mean perplexity saturates before tail statistics, placing a physical ceiling on EDD.
12	
13	\paragraph{Scale-dependent attenuation.}
14	Larger models approximate the training distribution more faithfully per step ($\alpha$ closer to 1), so EDD should decrease with scale.
15	Qwen2.5-14B confirms this: d0-vs-d1 AUC drops to 0.643 (vs.\ ${>}0.99$ at 1--1.5B), while d1--d3 AUCs remain above 0.70 (\S\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}).
16	
17	\paragraph{Strategy-dependent convergence.}
18	The contraction rate $\alpha$ varies with decoding strategy.
19	Temperature scaling ($\tau < 1$) sharpens the logit distribution uniformly and accelerates convergence to the mode; temperature depth-$d$ text should therefore resemble nucleus depth-$(d{+}1)$ text in tail statistics.
20	GPT-2~XL confirms this: temperature d1 median \texttt{p90\_ppl} (92.2) matches the nucleus d2 median (92.3, IQR 75.8--112.5).
21	Top-$k$ truncation removes the low-probability tokens that carry depth signal, predicting weaker discriminability; top-$k$ cells yield the lowest accuracy (68.9--75.8\%) across the atlas (\S\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).
22	
```

      "content": "\\subsection{Theoretical Grounding}\n\\label{sec:method:theory}\n\nRecursive generation contracts the support of the learned distribution, with low-probability tokens lost first \\cite{shumailov2024_nature}: tail mass $P_k(\\mathrm{ppl} > t)$ decreases monotonically in depth $k$.\nThis tail erosion predicts the perplexity tail feature family as a depth marker, confirmed by its dominance across all 9 cells (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}).\nSurprisal variance captures complementary distributional narrowing: as the support contracts, per-token surprise becomes more predictable.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD upper bound.}\nTail mass at depth $k$ scales as $\\alpha^k$ for a contraction factor $\\alpha \\in (0,1)$ \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}; EDD reaches its upper bound when adjacent-depth feature distance falls below classifier resolution.\nOur empirical AUC-excess contraction (ratio 0.80 at d1v2$\\to$d2v3, 0.69 at d3v4$\\to$d4v5; \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}) is consistent with geometric decay approaching a fixed point \\cite{bertrand2024_stability}.\n5-class extensions to d4 (71--80\\%, chance = 20\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:depth4_ext}) confirm depth~4 discriminability; mean perplexity saturates before tail statistics, placing a physical ceiling on EDD.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale-dependent attenuation.}\nLarger models approximate the training distribution more faithfully per step ($\\alpha$ closer to 1), so EDD should decrease with scale.\nQwen2.5-14B confirms this: d0-vs-d1 AUC drops to 0.643 (vs.\\ ${>}0.99$ at 1--1.5B), while d1--d3 AUCs remain above 0.70 (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossscale}).\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy-dependent convergence.}\nThe contraction rate $\\alpha$ varies with decoding strategy.\nTemperature scaling ($\\tau < 1$) sharpens the logit distribution uniformly and accelerates convergence to the mode; temperature depth-$d$ text should therefore resemble nucleus depth-$(d{+}1)$ text in tail statistics.\nGPT-2~XL confirms this: temperature d1 median \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (92.2) matches the nucleus d2 median (92.3, IQR 75.8--112.5).\nTop-$k$ truncation removes the low-probability tokens that carry depth signal, predicting weaker discriminability; top-$k$ cells yield the lowest accuracy (68.9--75.8\\%) across the atlas (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).\n",
      "numLines": 22,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 22
    }
  },

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Ljb2z1tpNdSUEdamzysScW`</summary>

```text
17	The sole 8B instruction-tuned model tested (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct) exhibits monotonically decreasing AUC, in contrast to the non-monotonic trajectory of its base-model counterpart; whether this regularization effect generalizes across architectures remains open (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_scale_full}).
18	
19	Following the tradition of benchmark-driven empirical studies in NLP, our contribution centers on task formalization and systematic empirical characterization rather than algorithmic novelty; the convergence of multiple classifiers (RF, XGBoost, LightGBM within 0.4\,pp; \S\ref{sec:gen:baselines}) confirms that discriminative signal resides in feature design, not classifier architecture.
20	We formalize recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification and find that a lightweight pipeline suffices: compute 19 reference-model statistical features (effective dimensionality ${\approx}$4--5 due to multicollinearity; \S\ref{sec:atlas:features}) and classify with a Random Forest into $K{+}1$ depth classes.
21	To characterize how recursive generation reshapes these features, we construct the \emph{Depth Degradation Atlas}: a $3 \times 3$ grid of model families $\times$ decoding strategies spanning 4 depths (180K samples).
```

</details>

  "uuid": "2facf40b-0b6b-47a2-9188-966b2449d37b",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "the convergence of multiple classifiers (RF, XGBoost, LightGBM within 0.4\\,pp; \\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}) confirms",
    "newString": "the convergence of four classifiers within 0.8\\,pp (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}) confirms",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:intro}\n\nThe text on which large language models are trained increasingly contains their own outputs.\nAs synthetic content proliferates across the web, a recursive loop emerges: models trained on web corpora ingest text generated by earlier models, and their outputs feed into the next generation's training data \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,alemohammad2024_mad}.\nThis model$\\to$data$\\to$model cycle is no longer hypothetical; it is already occurring in the wild. Yet whether the recursive generation depth of a text can be estimated from its statistical properties remains unstudied, a question that matters because first-generation synthetic text may be benign or even beneficial for training, while deeply recursive text drives the distributional collapse that degrades model quality \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}.\nIn this work, we focus on the 1--1.5B parameter regime under \\emph{controlled full-replacement chains}, where each generation fully replaces the prior training set, isolating the depth variable. Real-world contamination typically involves partial mixing (accumulation), where synthetic and human data co-exist at unknown proportions; the full-replacement setting provides an upper bound on depth signal strength, and we discuss implications for accumulation settings in \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n\nExisting tools address adjacent but distinct problems.\nBinary detectors such as Binoculars \\cite{binoculars2024}, Fast-DetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt2024}, and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026} classify text as human-written or machine-generated, collapsing all depths $d \\geq 1$ into a single category.\nWatermarking \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} requires proactive embedding and cannot be applied retroactively.\nThe model collapse literature \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} assumes depth is known a priori; \\citet{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse} filter synthetic data via binary detection without distinguishing depths.\nNo existing method estimates the ordinal recursive generation depth of a text passage from its content alone.\n\nWe find that recursive generation leaves quantifiable statistical fingerprints in text, and these fingerprints are \\textbf{consistent in direction but scale-dependent in magnitude} across the three model families and three decoding strategies studied.\nSpecifically, the perplexity tail feature family (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{p75\\_ppl}, \\texttt{kurtosis\\_ppl}) and surprisal variance emerge as dominant depth indicators, with group-level dominance robust across all 9 model--strategy configurations and three importance measures.\nThe sole 8B instruction-tuned model tested (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct) exhibits monotonically decreasing AUC, in contrast to the non-monotonic trajectory of its base-model counterpart; whether this regularization effect generalizes across architectures remains open (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}).\n\nFollowing the tradition of benchmark-driven empirical studies in NLP, our contribution centers on task formalization and systematic empirical characterization rather than algorithmic novelty; the convergence of multiple classifiers (RF, XGBoost, LightGBM within 0.4\\,pp; \\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}) confirms that discriminative signal resides in feature design, not classifier architecture.\nWe formalize recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification and find that a lightweight pipeline suffices: compute 19 reference-model statistical features (effective dimensionality ${\\approx}$4--5 due to multicollinearity; \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}) and classify with a Random Forest into $K{+}1$ depth classes.\nTo characterize how recursive generation reshapes these features, we construct the \\emph{Depth Degradation Atlas}: a $3 \\times 3$ grid of model families $\\times$ decoding strategies spanning 4 depths (180K samples).\nThe atlas reveals strategy-dependent degradation trajectories and we introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric to quantify how far into the recursion chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formalization and metric.} We define recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification, introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, and construct a 180K-sample benchmark ($3$ model families $\\times$ $3$ decoding strategies $\\times$ $4$ depths).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Depth Degradation Atlas.} The perplexity tail feature family dominates depth discrimination across all 9 cells; $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}4$ for all three families, validated by 5-class extension (71--80\\%); feature group ablation confirms perplexity tail dominance (76.6\\% alone vs.\\ 78.4\\% full set).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Systematic characterization.} Cross-model transfer 66.4\\% (d1--d3: 55.4\\%), cross-domain arXiv 74.1\\%, preliminary 14B 44.1\\% cross-reference ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}2$). We identify cross-strategy transfer (d1--d3: 45.9\\%) as a boundary condition: decoding strategies induce qualitatively distinct degradation trajectories, with strategy affecting transfer more than model architecture.\n\\end{itemize}",
    "structuredPatch": [

          " The sole 8B instruction-tuned model tested (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct) exhibits monotonically decreasing AUC, in contrast to the non-monotonic trajectory of its base-model counterpart; whether this regularization effect generalizes across architectures remains open (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}).",

    "newString": "Four classifiers (RF, XGBoost, ordinal MLP, prototypical) converge within 0.8\\,pp under cross-model transfer (Appendix~\\ref{app:learned_estimator}), confirming a feature-determined accuracy ceiling.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Generalization and Robustness}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nWe test whether atlas accuracy (68.9--85.9\\% four-class; d1--d3: 58.9--81.5\\%) reflects genuine statistical regularities across eight generalization axes (five below, three in Appendix).\n\n\\subsection{Classifier Comparison and Surface-Text Ablation}\n\\label{sec:gen:baselines}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} compares methods across all 9 cells.\nRF, XGBoost, and LightGBM converge within 0.4\\,pp (feature-determined ceiling); CORAL \\cite{coral2020} and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026} match within 0.6\\,pp on Pythia nucleus ($p{>}0.05$).\n\n\\paragraph{Neural end-to-end baselines.}\nDeBERTa-v3-base (LoRA) underperforms RF by 16--32\\,pp (d2 recall 8.4\\%); RoBERTa-DCOL (ordinal contrastive; 64.5\\%) narrows the gap but remains 14\\,pp below RF.\n\n\\paragraph{Binary detectors and membership inference.}\nPerturbation-based (DetectGPT, 28.8\\%) and membership-inference (Min-K\\% Prob, 33.2\\%; Min-K\\%++ \\cite{zhang2024_minkpp} comparable) methods produce scores with no ordinal resolution (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}); CORAL calibration does not close the gap (d1--d3: 40.4\\% vs.\\ RF 71.6\\%).\nBinoculars \\cite{binoculars2024}, calibrated with 11-feature RF, independently converges to 78.1\\% ($p{>}0.05$ vs.\\ our 78.4\\%); merging all 30 features yields 82.6\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_ablation_bino}).\n\n\\paragraph{Surface-text ablation.}\nSingle-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%); zero-shot LLMs (best 36.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:zeroshot_prompt}) also fail; depth signal resides in distributional statistics inaccessible from surface text.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Method comparison (RF mean across 9 cells, 5-fold CV). Chance = 25\\% / 33.3\\% (d1--d3). $^\\dagger$Adapted from binary detection; Binoculars 11-feat: $p{>}0.05$ vs.\\ 19-feat RF (paired permutation test).}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Calibration & 4-Cl (\\%) & d1--d3 (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF 19-feature (ours) & RF & 78.4 & \\textbf{71.6} \\\\\nBinoculars$^\\dagger$ \\cite{binoculars2024} & RF (11 feat) & 78.1 & --- \\\\\nMerged (30 feat)$^\\dagger$ & RF & \\textbf{82.6} & --- \\\\\nBinoculars \\cite{binoculars2024} & threshold & 69.8 & --- \\\\\nBinoculars \\cite{binoculars2024} & RF (1 feat) & 63.7 & --- \\\\\nRoBERTa-DCOL (ordinal) & contrastive & 64.5 & --- \\\\\nDeBERTa-v3 (end-to-end) & cross-entropy & 53.1--60.6 & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\nZero-shot LLM (best) & --- & 36.2 & --- \\\\\nMin-K\\% Prob \\cite{shi2024_minkprob} & CORAL & 33.2 & 40.4 \\\\\nDetectGPT \\cite{mitchell2023_detectgpt} & best-of-3 & 28.8 & 24.3 \\\\\nRandom & --- & 25.0 & 33.3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nConvergence confirms signal resides in the features: three classifiers match within 0.8\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:learned_estimator}), perplexity features alone recover 66.0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_replication}).\nFigure~\\ref{fig:confusion_matrix}: d0 recall ${>}$99\\%; errors concentrate at d2/d3 and spread to d1--d3 under transfer.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_confusion_matrix.pdf}\n\\caption{Confusion matrices: best in-distribution (GPT-2 XL temp, 85.9\\%) and best LOO transfer (OLMo, 69.2\\%). d0 recall ${>}$99\\%; errors spread to d1--d3 under transfer.}\n\\label{fig:confusion_matrix}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossmodel}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model} reports leave-one-model-family-out evaluation (nucleus only).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer (leave-one-out, nucleus only). Chance = 25\\% / 33\\% (d1--d3). d1--d3 mean 55.4\\%; QWK\\,=\\,0.81; 94.8\\% errors at $|\\Delta| \\leq 1$.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n & (\\%) & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia & 62.4 & 100.0 & 49.3 & 26.8 & 73.6 \\\\\nOLMo & \\textbf{69.2} & 99.3 & 75.5 & 43.3 & 58.8 \\\\\nGPT-2 XL & 67.6 & 98.5 & 74.7 & 52.6 & 44.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 66.4 & 99.3 & 66.5 & 40.9 & 59.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean transfer accuracy is 66.4\\% (d1--d3: 55.4\\%, $2.7{\\times}$ chance); d0 recall ($>$98\\%) inflates the headline; d2 worst case 26.8\\% (Pythia holdout).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossstrategy}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_strategy} reports leave-one-strategy-out transfer.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-strategy transfer (leave-one-out). Chance = 25\\% / 33\\% (d1--d3). d0 recall $\\geq$89\\% inflates headline; d1--d3 mean: 45.9\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_strategy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL \\\\\n & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nnucleus & \\textbf{69.4} & \\textbf{65.4} & \\textbf{59.9} \\\\\ntemp ($\\tau{=}0.9$) & 62.8 & 58.2 & 56.1 \\\\\ntop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 51.5 & 54.9 & 53.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nCross-strategy transfer reveals a structural barrier: d1--d3 balanced accuracy drops to 45.9\\% (four-class: 59.1\\%), 7\\,pp below cross-model transfer (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_strategy}).\nDecoding strategies induce qualitatively distinct degradation trajectories; a two-stage pipeline recovers 11.5\\,pp but presupposes known strategy (Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_discussion}).\nOur depth estimation claims hold most strongly within-strategy or when the decoding configuration is known.\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Preliminary Cross-Scale Observations (14B)}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossscale}\n\nAt 14B (Qwen2.5-14B), self-reference yields 50.8\\% ($2{\\times}$ chance, $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$); cross-reference control (Pythia-1.4B) drops accuracy to 44.1\\% ($-$6.8\\,pp) but raises $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}$ to 2 for nucleus and temperature, revealing ${\\sim}$7\\,pp of the apparent degradation is a reference-model artifact (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}).\nCross-domain (arXiv) accuracy reaches 74.1\\% at 1--1.5B; cross-domain FPR is discussed in Limitations.",

    "oldString": "Convergence confirms signal resides in the features: three classifiers match within 0.8\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:learned_estimator}), perplexity features alone recover 66.0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_replication}).",
    "newString": "Convergence confirms signal resides in the features: four classifiers match within 0.8\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:learned_estimator}), perplexity features alone recover 66.0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_replication}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Generalization and Robustness}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nWe test whether atlas accuracy (68.9--85.9\\% four-class; d1--d3: 58.9--81.5\\%) reflects genuine statistical regularities across eight generalization axes (five below, three in Appendix).\n\n\\subsection{Classifier Comparison and Surface-Text Ablation}\n\\label{sec:gen:baselines}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} compares methods across all 9 cells.\nFour classifiers (RF, XGBoost, ordinal MLP, prototypical) converge within 0.8\\,pp under cross-model transfer (Appendix~\\ref{app:learned_estimator}), confirming a feature-determined accuracy ceiling.\n\n\\paragraph{Neural end-to-end baselines.}\nDeBERTa-v3-base (LoRA) underperforms RF by 16--32\\,pp (d2 recall 8.4\\%); RoBERTa-DCOL (ordinal contrastive; 64.5\\%) narrows the gap but remains 14\\,pp below RF.\n\n\\paragraph{Binary detectors and membership inference.}\nPerturbation-based (DetectGPT, 28.8\\%) and membership-inference (Min-K\\% Prob, 33.2\\%; Min-K\\%++ \\cite{zhang2024_minkpp} comparable) methods produce scores with no ordinal resolution (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}); CORAL calibration does not close the gap (d1--d3: 40.4\\% vs.\\ RF 71.6\\%).\nBinoculars \\cite{binoculars2024}, calibrated with 11-feature RF, independently converges to 78.1\\% ($p{>}0.05$ vs.\\ our 78.4\\%); merging all 30 features yields 82.6\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_ablation_bino}).\n\n\\paragraph{Surface-text ablation.}\nSingle-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%); zero-shot LLMs (best 36.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:zeroshot_prompt}) also fail; depth signal resides in distributional statistics inaccessible from surface text.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Method comparison (RF mean across 9 cells, 5-fold CV). Chance = 25\\% / 33.3\\% (d1--d3). $^\\dagger$Adapted from binary detection; Binoculars 11-feat: $p{>}0.05$ vs.\\ 19-feat RF (paired permutation test).}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Calibration & 4-Cl (\\%) & d1--d3 (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF 19-feature (ours) & RF & 78.4 & \\textbf{71.6} \\\\\nBinoculars$^\\dagger$ \\cite{binoculars2024} & RF (11 feat) & 78.1 & --- \\\\\nMerged (30 feat)$^\\dagger$ & RF & \\textbf{82.6} & --- \\\\\nBinoculars \\cite{binoculars2024} & threshold & 69.8 & --- \\\\\nBinoculars \\cite{binoculars2024} & RF (1 feat) & 63.7 & --- \\\\\nRoBERTa-DCOL (ordinal) & contrastive & 64.5 & --- \\\\\nDeBERTa-v3 (end-to-end) & cross-entropy & 53.1--60.6 & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\nZero-shot LLM (best) & --- & 36.2 & --- \\\\\nMin-K\\% Prob \\cite{shi2024_minkprob} & CORAL & 33.2 & 40.4 \\\\\nDetectGPT \\cite{mitchell2023_detectgpt} & best-of-3 & 28.8 & 24.3 \\\\\nRandom & --- & 25.0 & 33.3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nConvergence confirms signal resides in the features: three classifiers match within 0.8\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:learned_estimator}), perplexity features alone recover 66.0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed_replication}).\nFigure~\\ref{fig:confusion_matrix}: d0 recall ${>}$99\\%; errors concentrate at d2/d3 and spread to d1--d3 under transfer.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_confusion_matrix.pdf}\n\\caption{Confusion matrices: best in-distribution (GPT-2 XL temp, 85.9\\%) and best LOO transfer (OLMo, 69.2\\%). d0 recall ${>}$99\\%; errors spread to d1--d3 under transfer.}\n\\label{fig:confusion_matrix}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossmodel}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model} reports leave-one-model-family-out evaluation (nucleus only).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer (leave-one-out, nucleus only). Chance = 25\\% / 33\\% (d1--d3). d1--d3 mean 55.4\\%; QWK\\,=\\,0.81; 94.8\\% errors at $|\\Delta| \\leq 1$.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n & (\\%) & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia & 62.4 & 100.0 & 49.3 & 26.8 & 73.6 \\\\\nOLMo & \\textbf{69.2} & 99.3 & 75.5 & 43.3 & 58.8 \\\\\nGPT-2 XL & 67.6 & 98.5 & 74.7 & 52.6 & 44.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 66.4 & 99.3 & 66.5 & 40.9 & 59.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean transfer accuracy is 66.4\\% (d1--d3: 55.4\\%, $2.7{\\times}$ chance); d0 recall ($>$98\\%) inflates the headline; d2 worst case 26.8\\% (Pythia holdout).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossstrategy}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_strategy} reports leave-one-strategy-out transfer.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-strategy transfer (leave-one-out). Chance = 25\\% / 33\\% (d1--d3). d0 recall $\\geq$89\\% inflates headline; d1--d3 mean: 45.9\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_strategy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL \\\\\n & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nnucleus & \\textbf{69.4} & \\textbf{65.4} & \\textbf{59.9} \\\\\ntemp ($\\tau{=}0.9$) & 62.8 & 58.2 & 56.1 \\\\\ntop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 51.5 & 54.9 & 53.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nCross-strategy transfer reveals a structural barrier: d1--d3 balanced accuracy drops to 45.9\\% (four-class: 59.1\\%), 7\\,pp below cross-model transfer (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_strategy}).\nDecoding strategies induce qualitatively distinct degradation trajectories; a two-stage pipeline recovers 11.5\\,pp but presupposes known strategy (Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_discussion}).\nOur depth estimation claims hold most strongly within-strategy or when the decoding configuration is known.\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Preliminary Cross-Scale Observations (14B)}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossscale}\n\nAt 14B (Qwen2.5-14B), self-reference yields 50.8\\% ($2{\\times}$ chance, $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}{=}0$); cross-reference control (Pythia-1.4B) drops accuracy to 44.1\\% ($-$6.8\\,pp) but raises $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.70}$ to 2 for nucleus and temperature, revealing ${\\sim}$7\\,pp of the apparent degradation is a reference-model artifact (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_scale_full}).\nCross-domain (arXiv) accuracy reaches 74.1\\% at 1--1.5B; cross-domain FPR is discussed in Limitations.",
